````math
## NeurIPS Paper Checklist

1.  1.
    Claims
2.
    Question: Do the main claims made in the abstract and introduction
    accurately reflect the paper’s contributions and scope?
3.
    Answer: \[Yes\]
4.
    Justification: The abstract and introduction precisely reflect the
    contribution and scope of this paper.
5.
    Guidelines:

    - •
      The answer NA means that the abstract and introduction do not
      include the claims made in the paper.
    - •
      The abstract and/or introduction should clearly state the claims
      made, including the contributions made in the paper and important
      assumptions and limitations. A No or NA answer to this question
      will not be perceived well by the reviewers.
    - •
      The claims made should match theoretical and experimental results,
      and reflect how much the results can be expected to generalize to
      other settings.
    - •
      It is fine to include aspirational goals as motivation as long as
      it is clear that these goals are not attained by the paper.
6.  2.
    Limitations
7.
    Question: Does the paper discuss the limitations of the work
    performed by the authors?
8.
    Answer: \[Yes\]
9.
    Justification: We discuss the limitations of our work in
    Section [6](#S6 "6 Conclusion ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning").
10.
    Guidelines:

    - •
      The answer NA means that the paper has no limitation while the
      answer No means that the paper has limitations, but those are not
      discussed in the paper.
    - •
      The authors are encouraged to create a separate "Limitations"
      section in their paper.
    - •
      The paper should point out any strong assumptions and how robust
      the results are to violations of these assumptions (e.g.,
      independence assumptions, noiseless settings, model
      well-specification, asymptotic approximations only holding
      locally). The authors should reflect on how these assumptions
      might be violated in practice and what the implications would be.
    - •
      The authors should reflect on the scope of the claims made, e.g.,
      if the approach was only tested on a few datasets or with a few
      runs. In general, empirical results often depend on implicit
      assumptions, which should be articulated.
    - •
      The authors should reflect on the factors that influence the
      performance of the approach. For example, a facial recognition
      algorithm may perform poorly when image resolution is low or
      images are taken in low lighting. Or a speech-to-text system might
      not be used reliably to provide closed captions for online
      lectures because it fails to handle technical jargon.
    - •
      The authors should discuss the computational efficiency of the
      proposed algorithms and how they scale with dataset size.
    - •
      If applicable, the authors should discuss possible limitations of
      their approach to address problems of privacy and fairness.
    - •
      While the authors might fear that complete honesty about
      limitations might be used by reviewers as grounds for rejection, a
      worse outcome might be that reviewers discover limitations that
      aren’t acknowledged in the paper. The authors should use their
      best judgment and recognize that individual actions in favor of
      transparency play an important role in developing norms that
      preserve the integrity of the community. Reviewers will be
      specifically instructed to not penalize honesty concerning
      limitations.
11. 3.
    Theory assumptions and proofs
12.
    Question: For each theoretical result, does the paper provide the
    full set of assumptions and a complete (and correct) proof?
13.
    Answer: \[Yes\]
14.
    Justification: We explicitly list all key Theorems in
    Section [4](#S4 "4 Performance Guarantees ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning"),
    and provide complete proofs in the Appendix.
15.
    Guidelines:

    - •
      The answer NA means that the paper does not include theoretical
      results.
    - •
      All the theorems, formulas, and proofs in the paper should be
      numbered and cross-referenced.
    - •
      All assumptions should be clearly stated or referenced in the
      statement of any theorems.
    - •
      The proofs can either appear in the main paper or the supplemental
      material, but if they appear in the supplemental material, the
      authors are encouraged to provide a short proof sketch to provide
      intuition.
    - •
      Inversely, any informal proof provided in the core of the paper
      should be complemented by formal proofs provided in appendix or
      supplemental material.
    - •
      Theorems and Lemmas that the proof relies upon should be properly
      referenced.
16. 4.
    Experimental result reproducibility
17.
    Question: Does the paper fully disclose all the information needed
    to reproduce the main experimental results of the paper to the
    extent that it affects the main claims and/or conclusions of the
    paper (regardless of whether the code and data are provided or not)?
18.
    Answer: \[Yes\]
19.
    Justification: We provide a detailed illustration of our proposed
    algorithm in the
    Appendix [A](#A1 "Appendix A Numerical Experiments ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning").
20.
    Guidelines:

    - •
      The answer NA means that the paper does not include experiments.
    - •
      If the paper includes experiments, a No answer to this question
      will not be perceived well by the reviewers: Making the paper
      reproducible is important, regardless of whether the code and data
      are provided or not.
    - •
      If the contribution is a dataset and/or model, the authors should
      describe the steps taken to make their results reproducible or
      verifiable.
    - •
      Depending on the contribution, reproducibility can be accomplished
      in various ways. For example, if the contribution is a novel
      architecture, describing the architecture fully might suffice, or
      if the contribution is a specific model and empirical evaluation,
      it may be necessary to either make it possible for others to
      replicate the model with the same dataset, or provide access to
      the model. In general. releasing code and data is often one good
      way to accomplish this, but reproducibility can also be provided
      via detailed instructions for how to replicate the results, access
      to a hosted model (e.g., in the case of a large language model),
      releasing of a model checkpoint, or other means that are
      appropriate to the research performed.
    - •
      While NeurIPS does not require releasing code, the conference does
      require all submissions to provide some reasonable avenue for
      reproducibility, which may depend on the nature of the
      contribution. For example

      1.  (a)
          If the contribution is primarily a new algorithm, the paper
          should make it clear how to reproduce that algorithm.
      2.  (b)
          If the contribution is primarily a new model architecture, the
          paper should describe the architecture clearly and fully.
      3.  (c)
          If the contribution is a new model (e.g., a large language
          model), then there should either be a way to access this model
          for reproducing the results or a way to reproduce the model
          (e.g., with an open-source dataset or instructions for how to
          construct the dataset).
      4.  (d)
          We recognize that reproducibility may be tricky in some cases,
          in which case authors are welcome to describe the particular
          way they provide for reproducibility. In the case of
          closed-source models, it may be that access to the model is
          limited in some way (e.g., to registered users), but it should
          be possible for other researchers to have some path to
          reproducing or verifying the results.
21. 5.
    Open access to data and code
22.
    Question: Does the paper provide open access to the data and code,
    with sufficient instructions to faithfully reproduce the main
    experimental results, as described in supplemental material?
23.
    Answer: \[Yes\]
24.
    Justification: Code is available at
    [https://github.com/NLee10/RTZ-VI-LCB](https://github.com/NLee10/RTZ-VI-LCB).
25.
    Guidelines:

    - •
      The answer NA means that paper does not include experiments
      requiring code.
    - •
      Please see the NeurIPS code and data submission guidelines
      ([https://nips.cc/public/guides/CodeSubmissionPolicy](https://nips.cc/public/guides/CodeSubmissionPolicy))
      for more details.
    - •
      While we encourage the release of code and data, we understand
      that this might not be possible, so “No” is an acceptable answer.
      Papers cannot be rejected simply for not including code, unless
      this is central to the contribution (e.g., for a new open-source
      benchmark).
    - •
      The instructions should contain the exact command and environment
      needed to run to reproduce the results. See the NeurIPS code and
      data submission guidelines
      ([https://nips.cc/public/guides/CodeSubmissionPolicy](https://nips.cc/public/guides/CodeSubmissionPolicy))
      for more details.
    - •
      The authors should provide instructions on data access and
      preparation, including how to access the raw data, preprocessed
      data, intermediate data, and generated data, etc.
    - •
      The authors should provide scripts to reproduce all experimental
      results for the new proposed method and baselines. If only a
      subset of experiments are reproducible, they should state which
      ones are omitted from the script and why.
    - •
      At submission time, to preserve anonymity, the authors should
      release anonymized versions (if applicable).
    - •
      Providing as much information as possible in supplemental material
      (appended to the paper) is recommended, but including URLs to data
      and code is permitted.
26. 6.
    Experimental setting/details
27.
    Question: Does the paper specify all the training and test details
    (e.g., data splits, hyperparameters, how they were chosen, type of
    optimizer, etc.) necessary to understand the results?
28.
    Answer: \[Yes\]
29.
    Justification: We provide full details in
    Appendix [A](#A1 "Appendix A Numerical Experiments ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning").
30.
    Guidelines:

    - •
      The answer NA means that the paper does not include experiments.
    - •
      The experimental setting should be presented in the core of the
      paper to a level of detail that is necessary to appreciate the
      results and make sense of them.
    - •
      The full details can be provided either with the code, in
      appendix, or as supplemental material.
31. 7.
    Experiment statistical significance
32.
    Question: Does the paper report error bars suitably and correctly
    defined or other appropriate information about the statistical
    significance of the experiments?
33.
    Answer: \[Yes\]
34.
    Justification: We report the average results across 100 seeds with
    standard deviations.
35.
    Guidelines:

    - •
      The answer NA means that the paper does not include experiments.
    - •
      The authors should answer "Yes" if the results are accompanied by
      error bars, confidence intervals, or statistical significance
      tests, at least for the experiments that support the main claims
      of the paper.
    - •
      The factors of variability that the error bars are capturing
      should be clearly stated (for example, train/test split,
      initialization, random drawing of some parameter, or overall run
      with given experimental conditions).
    - •
      The method for calculating the error bars should be explained
      (closed form formula, call to a library function, bootstrap, etc.)
    - •
      The assumptions made should be given (e.g., Normally distributed
      errors).
    - •
      It should be clear whether the error bar is the standard deviation
      or the standard error of the mean.
    - •
      It is OK to report 1-sigma error bars, but one should state it.
      The authors should preferably report a 2-sigma error bar than
      state that they have a 96% CI, if the hypothesis of Normality of
      errors is not verified.
    - •
      For asymmetric distributions, the authors should be careful not to
      show in tables or figures symmetric error bars that would yield
      results that are out of range (e.g. negative error rates).
    - •
      If error bars are reported in tables or plots, The authors should
      explain in the text how they were calculated and reference the
      corresponding figures or tables in the text.
36. 8.
    Experiments compute resources
37.
    Question: For each experiment, does the paper provide sufficient
    information on the computer resources (type of compute workers,
    memory, time of execution) needed to reproduce the experiments?
38.
    Answer: \[Yes\]
39.
    Justification: We point out the specific compute resources in
    Appendix [A](#A1 "Appendix A Numerical Experiments ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning").
40.
    Guidelines:

    - •
      The answer NA means that the paper does not include experiments.
    - •
      The paper should indicate the type of compute workers CPU or GPU,
      internal cluster, or cloud provider, including relevant memory and
      storage.
    - •
      The paper should provide the amount of compute required for each
      of the individual experimental runs as well as estimate the total
      compute.
    - •
      The paper should disclose whether the full research project
      required more compute than the experiments reported in the paper
      (e.g., preliminary or failed experiments that didn’t make it into
      the paper).
41. 9.
    Code of ethics
42.
    Question: Does the research conducted in the paper conform, in every
    respect, with the NeurIPS Code of Ethics
    [https://neurips.cc/public/EthicsGuidelines](https://neurips.cc/public/EthicsGuidelines)?
43.
    Answer: \[Yes\]
44.
    Justification: Our paper obeys the NeurIPS Code of Ethics.
45.
    Guidelines:

    - •
      The answer NA means that the authors have not reviewed the NeurIPS
      Code of Ethics.
    - •
      If the authors answer No, they should explain the special
      circumstances that require a deviation from the Code of Ethics.
    - •
      The authors should make sure to preserve anonymity (e.g., if there
      is a special consideration due to laws or regulations in their
      jurisdiction).
46. 10.
    Broader impacts
47.
    Question: Does the paper discuss both potential positive societal
    impacts and negative societal impacts of the work performed?
48.
    Answer: \[No\]
49.
    Justification: There is no societal impact of the work performed
    since this work focuses on theoretical analysis.
50.
    Guidelines:

    - •
      The answer NA means that there is no societal impact of the work
      performed.
    - •
      If the authors answer NA or No, they should explain why their work
      has no societal impact or why the paper does not address societal
      impact.
    - •
      Examples of negative societal impacts include potential malicious
      or unintended uses (e.g., disinformation, generating fake
      profiles, surveillance), fairness considerations (e.g., deployment
      of technologies that could make decisions that unfairly impact
      specific groups), privacy considerations, and security
      considerations.
    - •
      The conference expects that many papers will be foundational
      research and not tied to particular applications, let alone
      deployments. However, if there is a direct path to any negative
      applications, the authors should point it out. For example, it is
      legitimate to point out that an improvement in the quality of
      generative models could be used to generate deepfakes for
      disinformation. On the other hand, it is not needed to point out
      that a generic algorithm for optimizing neural networks could
      enable people to train models that generate Deepfakes faster.
    - •
      The authors should consider possible harms that could arise when
      the technology is being used as intended and functioning
      correctly, harms that could arise when the technology is being
      used as intended but gives incorrect results, and harms following
      from (intentional or unintentional) misuse of the technology.
    - •
      If there are negative societal impacts, the authors could also
      discuss possible mitigation strategies (e.g., gated release of
      models, providing defenses in addition to attacks, mechanisms for
      monitoring misuse, mechanisms to monitor how a system learns from
      feedback over time, improving the efficiency and accessibility of
      ML).

## Appendix A Numerical Experiments

| $`\log(KH)`$                                | $`6`$  | $`7`$  | $`8`$  | $`9`$  |
|---------------------------------------------|--------|--------|--------|--------|
| RTZ-VI-LCB                                  | 3.1699 | 2.1498 | 0.2324 | 0.0819 |
| $`\mathrm{P}^{2}\mathrm{M}^{2}\mathrm{PO}`$ | 3.5970 | 2.2503 | 0.2404 | 0.0890 |

These results highlight the superiority of our method across varying
dataset sizes and validate the theoretical claims under controlled
experimental conditions. While we focus on tabular settings in this
work, our algorithm can serve as a theoretical foundation for scalable
extensions based on linear or kernel-based function approximation.

## Appendix B Preliminaries

In this appendix, we introduce the dual equivalence of robust Bellman
operators and key facts about RTZMGs and their empirical counterparts,
laying the foundation for our theoretical analysis.

### B.1 Dual equivalence of robust Bellman

We can compute the robust Bellman operator by solving its dual
formulation rather than the original form, as long as the predefined
uncertainty set is in a benign form (e.g., utilizing TV distance as the
divergence function) \[[17](#bib.bib1), [33](#bib.bib14)\]. Taking TV
distance as an example, we describe the equivalence under strong duality
between the robust Bellman operator and its dual form as
Lemma [B.1](#A2.Thmtheorem1 "Lemma B.1. ‣ B.1 Dual equivalence of robust Bellman ‣ Appendix B Preliminaries ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning").

###### Lemma B.1.

Consider any TV uncertainty set $`\mathcal{U}^{\sigma^{+}}(P)`$ and
$`\mathcal{U}^{\sigma^{-}}(P)`$ associated with fixed uncertainty levels
$`\sigma^{+},\sigma^{-}\in(0,1]`$ and any probability vector
$`P\in\Delta(\mathcal{S})`$. For any vector $`V\in\mathbb{R}^{S}`$
obeying $`V\geq{0}`$, one has

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  |  |  |  |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle\inf_{P\in\mathcal{U}^{\sigma^{+}}(P)}PV`$ | $`\displaystyle=\max_{\alpha\in[\min_{s}V(s),\max_{s}V(s)]}\left\{P\left[V\right]_{\alpha}-\sigma^{+}~\left(\alpha-\min_{s^{\prime}}\left[V\right]_{\alpha}(s^{\prime})\right)\right\};`$ |  | (27a) |
|  | $`\displaystyle\sup_{P\in\mathcal{U}^{\sigma^{-}}(P)}PV`$ | $`\displaystyle=\min_{\alpha\in[\min_{s}V(s),\max_{s}V(s)]}\left\{P\left[V\right]_{\alpha}-\sigma^{-}~\left(\alpha-\max_{s^{\prime}}\left[V\right]_{\alpha}(s^{\prime})\right)\right\},`$ |  | (27b) |

where $`[V]_{\alpha}`$ is defined in
([17](#S3.E17 "In Dual problem. ‣ 3.2 Optimistic Variant of Robust Value Iteration with Lower Confidence Bounds ‣ 3 Algorithm Design ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning"))

Lemma [B.1](#A2.Thmtheorem1 "Lemma B.1. ‣ B.1 Dual equivalence of robust Bellman ‣ Appendix B Preliminaries ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning")
can be proved similarly to Lemma 4.3 in \[[17](#bib.bib1)\]. Compared
the standard Bellman operator, this lemma guarantees that no additional
computing cost is required when applying the robust Bellman operator.

### B.2 Facts of RTZMGs and empirical RTZMGs

Recall the definition of any RTZMG
$`\mathcal{MG}_{\mathsf{r}}=\big\{\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{U}^{\sigma^{+}}_{\rho}(P^{0}),\mathcal{U}^{\sigma^{-}}_{\rho}(P^{0}),r,H\big\}`$.
According to robust Bellman equations in
([7](#S2.E7 "In Robust Bellman equations. ‣ 2 Problem Formulation ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning")),
one has: for any product policy $`(\mu,\nu)`$ and any
$`(h,s,a,b)\in[H]\times\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}`$,

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  |  |  |  |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle Q_{h}^{\mu,\nu,\sigma^{+}}(s,a,b)`$ | $`\displaystyle=r_{h}(s,a,b)+\inf_{P\in\mathcal{U}^{\sigma^{+}}_{\rho}(P^{0}_{h,s,a,b})}PV_{h+1}^{\mu,\nu,\sigma^{+}};`$ |  | (28a) |
|  | $`\displaystyle Q_{h}^{\mu,\nu,\sigma^{-}}(s,a,b)`$ | $`\displaystyle=r_{h}(s,a,b)+\sup_{P\in\mathcal{U}^{\sigma^{-}}_{\rho}(P^{0}_{h,s,a,b})}PV_{h+1}^{\mu,\nu,\sigma^{-}},`$ |  | (28b) |

where

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle V_{h}^{\mu,\nu,\sigma^{+}}(s)`$ | $`\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{a\sim\mu_{h}(s),b\sim\nu_{h}(s)}\left[Q^{\mu,\nu,\sigma^{+}}_{h}(s,a,b)\right];`$ |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle V_{h}^{\mu,\nu,\sigma^{-}}(s)`$ | $`\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{a\sim\mu_{h}(s),b\sim\nu_{h}(s)}\left[Q^{\mu,\nu,\sigma^{-}}_{h}(s,a,b)\right].`$ |  |

Considering the offline setting, we use
$`\widehat{\mathcal{MG}}_{\mathsf{r}}=\big\{\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{U}^{\sigma^{+}}_{\rho}(\widehat{P}^{0}),\mathcal{U}^{\sigma^{-}}_{\rho}(\widehat{P}^{0}),r,H\big\}`$
to represent the empirical RTZMG, which is established along with the
estimated nominal distribution $`\widehat{P}^{0}`$
in ([10](#S3.E10 "In 3.1 Building an Empirical Nominal MDP ‣ 3 Algorithm Design ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning")).
Therefore, for any product policy $`(\mu,\nu)`$, we define the empirical
robust value function (resp. empirical robust Q-function) in
$`\widehat{\mathcal{MG}}_{\mathsf{r}}`$ as
$`\widehat{V}_{h}^{\mu,\nu,\sigma^{+}}`$ and
$`\widehat{V}_{h}^{\mu,\nu,\sigma^{-}}`$
(resp. $`\widehat{Q}_{h}^{\mu,\nu,\sigma^{+}}`$ and
$`\widehat{Q}_{h}^{\mu,\nu,\sigma^{-}}`$), which are analogous to
([6](#S2.E6 "In Robust value functions. ‣ 2 Problem Formulation ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning")).
Moreover, we can similarly define the optimal empirical robust value
function for both players over $`\widehat{\mathcal{MG}}_{\mathsf{r}}`$:
$`\forall s\in\mathcal{S}`$,

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  |  |  |  |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle\widehat{V}_{h}^{\star,\nu,\sigma^{+}}(s)=\widehat{V}_{h}^{\mu^{\star},\nu,\sigma^{+}}(s)`$ | $`\displaystyle\coloneqq\max_{\mu:\mathcal{S}\times[H]\rightarrow\Delta(\mathcal{A})}\widehat{V}_{h}^{\mu,\nu,\sigma^{+}}(s)=\max_{\mu:\mathcal{S}\times[H]\rightarrow\Delta(\mathcal{A})}\inf_{P\in\mathcal{U}^{\sigma^{+}}(\widehat{P}^{0})}\widehat{V}_{h}^{\mu,\nu,P}(s);`$ |  | (29a) |
|  | $`\displaystyle\widehat{V}_{h}^{\mu,\star,\sigma^{-}}(s)=\widehat{V}_{h}^{\mu,\nu^{\star},\sigma^{-}}(s)`$ | $`\displaystyle\coloneqq\max_{\nu:\mathcal{S}\times[H]\rightarrow\Delta(\mathcal{B})}\widehat{V}_{h}^{\mu,\nu,\sigma^{-}}(s)=\max_{\nu:\mathcal{S}\times[H]\rightarrow\Delta(\mathcal{B})}\inf_{P\in\mathcal{U}^{\sigma^{-}}(\widehat{P}^{0})}\widehat{V}_{h}^{\mu,\nu,P}(s).`$ |  | (29b) |

Notably, for all $`s\in\mathcal{S}`$, there exists at least one robust
best-response policy that can achieve
$`\widehat{V}_{h}^{\star,\nu,\sigma^{+}}(s)`$ and
$`\widehat{V}_{h}^{\mu,\star,\sigma^{-}}(s)`$, as proved in
\[[5](#bib.bib29)\]. Therefore, we can obtain the empirical robust
Bellman equation similar to
([7](#S2.E7 "In Robust Bellman equations. ‣ 2 Problem Formulation ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning"))
as: for any product policy $`(\mu,\nu)`$,

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  |  |  |  |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle\widehat{Q}_{h}^{\mu,\nu,\sigma^{+}}(s,a,b)`$ | $`\displaystyle=r_{h}(s,a,b)+\inf_{P\in\mathcal{U}^{\sigma^{+}}_{\rho}(\widehat{P}^{0}_{h,s,a,b})}P\widehat{V}_{h+1}^{\mu,\nu,\sigma^{+}};`$ |  | (30a) |
|  | $`\displaystyle\widehat{Q}_{h}^{\mu,\nu,\sigma^{-}}(s,a,b)`$ | $`\displaystyle=r_{h}(s,a,b)+\sup_{P\in\mathcal{U}^{\sigma^{-}}_{\rho}(\widehat{P}^{0}_{h,s,a,b})}P\widehat{V}_{h+1}^{\mu,\nu,\sigma^{-}},`$ |  | (30b) |

where

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\widehat{V}_{h}^{\mu,\nu,\sigma^{+}}(s)`$ | $`\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{a\sim\mu_{h}(s),b\sim\nu_{h}(s)}[\widehat{Q}^{\mu,\nu,\sigma^{+}}_{h}(s,a,b)];`$ |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle\widehat{V}_{h}^{\mu,\nu,\sigma^{-}}(s)`$ | $`\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{a\sim\mu_{h}(s),b\sim\nu_{h}(s)}[\widehat{Q}^{\mu,\nu,\sigma^{-}}_{h}(s,a,b)].`$ |  |

## Appendix C Proof of Lemma [3.1](#S3.Thmtheorem1 "Lemma 3.1. ‣ 3.1 Building an Empirical Nominal MDP ‣ 3 Algorithm Design ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning")

### C.1 Independence property

Let us examine two distinct data-generation mechanisms, where a sample
transition quadruple $`(s,a,b,h,s^{\prime})`$ represents a transition
from state $`s`$ with actions $`(a,b)`$ to state $`s^{\prime}`$ at step
$`h`$.

##### Step 1: Creating $`\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{t,a}}`$ based on $`\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{t}}`$.

To construct the augmented dataset $`\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{t,a}}`$, for
each $`(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}\times[H]`$, we first include all
$`N^{\mathrm{t}}_{h}(s)`$ sample transitions in
$`\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{t}}`$ originating from state $`s`$ at step $`h`$
in $`\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{t,a}}`$. If
$`N^{\mathrm{t}}_{h}(s)>N^{\mathrm{m}}_{h}(s)`$, we supplement
$`\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{t,a}}`$ with additional
$`[N^{\mathrm{t}}_{h}(s)-N^{\mathrm{m}}_{h}(s)]`$ independent sample
transitions
$`\big\{\big(s,a^{(i)}_{h,s},b^{(i)}_{h,s},h,s^{\prime\,(i)}_{h,s}\big)\big\}`$,
as follows:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
a^{(i)}_{h,s}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mu^{\mathrm{b}}_{h}(\cdot|s),\quad b^{(i)}_{h,s}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\nu^{\mathrm{b}}_{h}(\cdot|s),\quad s^{\prime\,(i)}_{h,s}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}P_{h}\big(\cdot|s,a^{(i)}_{h,s},b^{(i)}_{h,s}\big),\quad N^{\mathrm{m}}_{h}(s)<i\leq N^{\mathrm{t}}_{h}(s).
``` |  |

##### Step 2: Constructing $`\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{iid}}`$.

For each $`(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}\times[H]`$, we generate
$`N^{\mathrm{t}}_{h}(s)`$ independent sample transitions
$`\big\{\big(s,a^{(i)}_{h,s},b^{(i)}_{h,s},h,s^{\prime\,(i)}_{h,s}\big)\big\}`$,
as follows:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
a^{(i)}_{h,s}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mu^{\mathrm{b}}_{h}(\cdot|s),\quad b^{(i)}_{h,s}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\nu^{\mathrm{b}}_{h}(\cdot|s),\quad s^{\prime\,(i)}_{h,s}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}P_{h}\big(\cdot|s,a,b\big),\quad 1\leq i\leq N^{\mathrm{t}}_{h}(s).
``` |  |

The resulting dataset is defined as:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{iid}}\coloneqq\Big\{\big(s,a^{(i)}_{h,s},b^{(i)}_{h,s},h,s^{\prime\,(i)}_{h,s}\big)\mid s\in\mathcal{S},1\leq h\leq H,1\leq i\leq N^{\mathrm{t}}_{h}(s)\Big\}.
``` |  |

##### Establishing independence property.

The dataset $`\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{t,a}}`$ deviates from
$`\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{t}}`$ only if
$`N^{\mathrm{t}}_{h}(s)>N^{\mathrm{m}}_{h}(s)`$. This augmentation
ensures that $`\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{t,a}}`$ contains precisely
$`N^{\mathrm{t}}_{h}(s)`$ sample transitions from state $`s`$ at step
$`h`$. Both $`\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{t,a}}`$ and
$`\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{iid}}`$ comprise exactly
$`N^{\mathrm{t}}_{h}(s)`$ sample transitions from state $`s`$ at step
$`h`$, with $`\{N^{\mathrm{t}}_{h}(s)\}`$ being statistically
independent of random sample generation. Consequently, given
$`\{N^{\mathrm{t}}_{h}(s)\}`$, the sample transitions in
$`\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{t,a}}`$ across different steps are statistically
independent. As a result, both $`\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{t}}`$ and
$`\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{iid}}`$ can be regarded as collections of
independent samples.

### C.2 Proof of $`N^{\mathsf{t}}_{h}(s)\leq N^{\mathsf{m}}_{h}(s)`$

Since $`\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{a}}`$ is generated by half of the sample
trajectories in line 2 in
Algorithm [1](#alg1 "Algorithm 1 ‣ 3.1 Building an Empirical Nominal MDP ‣ 3 Algorithm Design ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning"),
we have

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
N^{\mathsf{a}}_{h}(s)=\sum_{k=K/2+1}^{K}\mathds{1}\big\{s_{h}^{k}=s\big\},\,\forall s\in\mathcal{S},1\leq h\leq H.
``` |  |

Thus, we can view $`N^{\mathsf{a}}_{h}(s)`$ as the sum of $`K/2`$
independent Bernoulli random variables with mean
$`d^{\mu^{\mathsf{n}},\nu^{\mathsf{n}}}_{h}(s)`$. According to the
Bernstein inequality and the union bound, we derive

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left\{\exists(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}\times[H]:\left|N^{\mathsf{a}}_{h}(s)-\frac{K}{2}d^{\mu^{\mathsf{n}},\nu^{\mathsf{n}}}_{h}(s)\right|\geq N_{0}\right\}`$ |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle\leq`$ | $`\displaystyle\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S},h\in[H]}\mathbb{P}\left\{\left|N^{\mathsf{a}}_{h}(s)-\frac{K}{2}d^{\mu^{\mathsf{n}},\nu^{\mathsf{n}}}_{h}(s)\right|\geq N_{0}\right\}`$ |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle\leq`$ | $`\displaystyle 2HS\exp\left(-\frac{N_{0}^{2}/2}{N_{h,s}+N_{0}/3}\right),\quad\forall N_{0}\geq 0,`$ |  |

where

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
N_{h,s}\coloneqq\frac{K}{2}\mathsf{Var}\big(\mathds{1}\{s_{h}^{t}=s\}\big)=\frac{Kd^{\mu^{\mathsf{n}},\nu^{\mathsf{n}}}_{h}(s)\big(1-d^{\mu^{\mathsf{n}},\nu^{\mathsf{n}}}_{h}(s)\big)}{2}\leq\frac{Kd^{\mu^{\mathsf{n}},\nu^{\mathsf{n}}}_{h}(s)}{2}.
``` |  |

Therefore, with probability of at least $`1-2\delta`$, we have that:
$`\forall s\in\mathcal{S}`$ and $`\forall 1\leq h\leq H`$,

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\left|N^{\mathsf{a}}_{h}(s)-\frac{K}{2}d^{\mu^{\mathsf{n}},\nu^{\mathsf{n}}}_{h}(s)\right|`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{4N_{h,s}\log\frac{HS}{\delta}}+\frac{2}{3}\log\frac{HS}{\delta}\leq\sqrt{2Kd^{\mu^{\mathsf{n}},\nu^{\mathsf{n}}}_{h}(s)\log\frac{HS}{\delta}}+\log\frac{HS}{\delta}.`$ |  | (31) |

Since $`\mathcal{D}^{\sf{m}}`$ and $`\mathcal{D}^{\sf{a}}`$ are
generated in the same way, we obtain that with probability exceeding
$`1-2\delta`$,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\left|N^{\mathsf{m}}_{h}(s)-\frac{K}{2}d^{\mu^{\mathsf{n}},\nu^{\mathsf{n}}}_{h}(s)\right|\leq\sqrt{2Kd^{\mu^{\mathsf{n}},\nu^{\mathsf{n}}}_{h}(s)\log\frac{HS}{\delta}}+\log\frac{HS}{\delta},\,\forall s\in\mathcal{S},1\leq h\leq H.`$ |  | (32) |

By combining
([31](#A3.E31 "In C.2 Proof of 𝑁^𝗍_ℎ⁢(𝑠)≤𝑁^𝗆_ℎ⁢(𝑠) ‣ Appendix C Proof of Lemma ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning"))
and
([32](#A3.E32 "In C.2 Proof of 𝑁^𝗍_ℎ⁢(𝑠)≤𝑁^𝗆_ℎ⁢(𝑠) ‣ Appendix C Proof of Lemma ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning")),
it follows that

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\left|N^{\mathsf{m}}_{h}(s)-N^{\mathsf{a}}_{h}(s)\right|\leq 2\sqrt{2Kd^{\mu^{\mathsf{n}},\nu^{\mathsf{n}}}_{h}(s)\log\frac{HS}{\delta}}+2\log\frac{HS}{\delta},\,\forall s\in\mathcal{S},1\leq h\leq H.`$ |  | (33) |

Now, we prove $`N^{\mathsf{t}}_{h}(s)\leq N^{\mathsf{m}}_{h}(s)`$ by
considering two cases. In the first case,
$`N^{\mathsf{a}}_{h}(s)\leq 100\log\frac{HS}{\delta}`$. According to the
definition in
([13](#S3.E13 "In 2 ‣ Algorithm 1 ‣ 3.1 Building an Empirical Nominal MDP ‣ 3 Algorithm Design ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning")),
we obtain

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle N^{\mathsf{t}}_{h}(s)=\max\left\{N^{\mathsf{a}}_{h}(s)-10\sqrt{N^{\mathsf{a}}_{h}(s)\log\frac{HS}{\delta}},\,0\right\}=0\leq N^{\mathsf{m}}_{h}(s).`$ |  | (34) |

In the second case, $`N^{\mathsf{a}}_{h}(s)>100\log\frac{HS}{\delta}`$.
From
([31](#A3.E31 "In C.2 Proof of 𝑁^𝗍_ℎ⁢(𝑠)≤𝑁^𝗆_ℎ⁢(𝑠) ‣ Appendix C Proof of Lemma ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning")),
it follows that

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\frac{K}{2}d^{\mu^{\mathsf{n}},\nu^{\mathsf{n}}}_{h}(s)+\sqrt{2Kd^{\mu^{\mathsf{n}},\nu^{\mathsf{n}}}_{h}(s)\log\frac{HS}{\delta}}+\log\frac{HS}{\delta}\geq N^{\mathsf{a}}_{h}(s),
``` |  |

which leads to

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle Kd^{\mu^{\mathsf{n}},\nu^{\mathsf{n}}}_{h}(s)\geq(9\sqrt{2})^{2}\log\frac{HS}{\delta}\geq 100\log\frac{HS}{\delta}.`$ |  | (35) |

By substituting
([35](#A3.E35 "In C.2 Proof of 𝑁^𝗍_ℎ⁢(𝑠)≤𝑁^𝗆_ℎ⁢(𝑠) ‣ Appendix C Proof of Lemma ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning"))
into
([31](#A3.E31 "In C.2 Proof of 𝑁^𝗍_ℎ⁢(𝑠)≤𝑁^𝗆_ℎ⁢(𝑠) ‣ Appendix C Proof of Lemma ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning")),
we obtain

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle N^{\mathsf{a}}_{h}(s)\geq\frac{K}{2}d^{\mu^{\mathsf{n}},\nu^{\mathsf{n}}}_{h}(s)-\sqrt{2Kd^{\mu^{\mathsf{n}},\nu^{\mathsf{n}}}_{h}(s)\log\frac{HS}{\delta}}-\log\frac{HS}{\delta}\geq\frac{K}{4}d^{\mu^{\mathsf{n}},\nu^{\mathsf{n}}}_{h}(s).`$ |  | (36) |

Consequently, in the case of
$`N^{\mathsf{a}}_{h}(s)>100\log\frac{HS}{\delta}`$, we have

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle N^{\mathsf{t}}_{h}(s)`$ | $`\displaystyle=\max\left\{N^{\mathsf{a}}_{h}(s)-10\sqrt{N^{\mathsf{a}}_{h}(s)\log\frac{HS}{\delta}},\,0\right\}`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=N^{\mathsf{a}}_{h}(s)-10\sqrt{N^{\mathsf{a}}_{h}(s)\log\frac{HS}{\delta}}`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(i)}}{\leq}N^{\mathsf{a}}_{h}(s)-5\sqrt{Kd^{\mu^{\mathsf{n}},\nu^{\mathsf{n}}}_{h}(s)\log\frac{HS}{\delta}}`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(ii)}}{\leq}N^{\mathsf{a}}_{h}(s)-\left\{2\sqrt{2Kd^{\mu^{\mathsf{n}},\nu^{\mathsf{n}}}_{h}(s)\log\frac{HS}{\delta}}+2\log\frac{HS}{\delta}\right\}\overset{\mathrm{(iii)}}{\leq}N^{\mathsf{m}}_{h}(s),`$ |  | (37) |

where (i) holds under
condition ([36](#A3.E36 "In C.2 Proof of 𝑁^𝗍_ℎ⁢(𝑠)≤𝑁^𝗆_ℎ⁢(𝑠) ‣ Appendix C Proof of Lemma ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning")),
(ii) holds under the condition
([35](#A3.E35 "In C.2 Proof of 𝑁^𝗍_ℎ⁢(𝑠)≤𝑁^𝗆_ℎ⁢(𝑠) ‣ Appendix C Proof of Lemma ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning")),
and (iii) comes from the inequality
([33](#A3.E33 "In C.2 Proof of 𝑁^𝗍_ℎ⁢(𝑠)≤𝑁^𝗆_ℎ⁢(𝑠) ‣ Appendix C Proof of Lemma ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning"))
with probability of at least $`1-2\delta`$.

Combining
([34](#A3.E34 "In C.2 Proof of 𝑁^𝗍_ℎ⁢(𝑠)≤𝑁^𝗆_ℎ⁢(𝑠) ‣ Appendix C Proof of Lemma ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning"))
and
([37](#A3.E37 "In C.2 Proof of 𝑁^𝗍_ℎ⁢(𝑠)≤𝑁^𝗆_ℎ⁢(𝑠) ‣ Appendix C Proof of Lemma ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning")),
we establish $`N^{\mathsf{t}}_{h}(s)\leq N^{\mathsf{m}}_{h}(s)`$.

As proved in
Appendix [C.3](#A3.SS3 "C.3 Proof of (). ‣ Appendix C Proof of Lemma ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning"),
we have:
$`\forall(s,a,b,h)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}\times[H]`$,
with probability exceeding $`1-2\delta`$,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle N^{\mathsf{t}}_{h}(s,a,b)\geq N^{\mathsf{t}}_{h}(s)\mu_{h}^{\mathsf{n}}(a\,|\,s)\nu_{h}^{\mathsf{n}}(b\,|\,s)-\sqrt{4N^{\mathsf{t}}_{h}(s)\mu_{h}^{\mathsf{n}}(a\,|\,s)\nu_{h}^{\mathsf{n}}(b\,|\,s)\log\frac{KH}{\delta}}-\log\frac{KH}{\delta}.`$ |  | (38) |

Employing the fact $`N^{\mathsf{t}}_{h}(s)\leq N^{\mathsf{m}}_{h}(s)`$
and
([38](#A3.E38 "In C.2 Proof of 𝑁^𝗍_ℎ⁢(𝑠)≤𝑁^𝗆_ℎ⁢(𝑠) ‣ Appendix C Proof of Lemma ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning")),
we can prove
([14](#S3.E14 "In Lemma 3.1. ‣ 3.1 Building an Empirical Nominal MDP ‣ 3 Algorithm Design ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning"))
in two cases, i.e.,
$`Kd^{\mu^{\mathsf{n}},\nu^{\mathsf{n}}}_{h}(s,a,b)\leq 1600\log\frac{KH}{\delta}`$
and
$`Kd^{\mu^{\mathsf{n}},\nu^{\mathsf{n}}}_{h}(s,a,b)>1600\log\frac{KH}{\delta}`$.

In the case of
$`Kd^{\mu^{\mathsf{n}},\nu^{\mathsf{n}}}_{h}(s,a)\leq 1600\log\frac{KH}{\delta}`$,
we can readily obtain

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\frac{K}{8}d^{\mu^{\mathsf{n}},\nu^{\mathsf{n}}}_{h}(s,a)-5\sqrt{Kd^{\mu^{\mathsf{n}},\nu^{\mathsf{n}}}_{h}(s,a)\log\frac{KH}{\delta}}\leq 0\leq N^{\mathsf{t}}_{h}(s,a).`$ |  | (39) |

In the case of
$`Kd^{\mu^{\mathsf{n}},\nu^{\mathsf{n}}}_{h}(s,a,b)=Kd^{\mu^{\mathsf{n}},\nu^{\mathsf{n}}}_{h}(s)\mu_{h}^{\mathsf{n}}(a\,|\,s)\nu_{h}^{\mathsf{n}}(b\,|\,s)>1600\log\frac{KH}{\delta}`$,
we obtain

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle N^{\mathsf{a}}_{h}(s)\geq\frac{K}{4}d^{\mu^{\mathsf{n}},\nu^{\mathsf{n}}}_{h}(s)\geq 400\log\frac{KH}{\delta},`$ |  | (40) |

which can be derived following the same line of
([36](#A3.E36 "In C.2 Proof of 𝑁^𝗍_ℎ⁢(𝑠)≤𝑁^𝗆_ℎ⁢(𝑠) ‣ Appendix C Proof of Lemma ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning")).
Then,
([40](#A3.E40 "In C.2 Proof of 𝑁^𝗍_ℎ⁢(𝑠)≤𝑁^𝗆_ℎ⁢(𝑠) ‣ Appendix C Proof of Lemma ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning"))
and the definition of $`N^{\mathsf{t}}_{h}(s)`$ together yield

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle N^{\mathsf{t}}_{h}(s)`$ | $`\displaystyle\geq N^{\mathsf{a}}_{h}(s)-10\sqrt{N^{\mathsf{a}}_{h}(s)\log\frac{KH}{\delta}}`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\geq\frac{K}{4}d^{\mu^{\mathsf{n}},\nu^{\mathsf{n}}}_{h}(s)-10\sqrt{\frac{K}{4}d^{\mu^{\mathsf{n}},\nu^{\mathsf{n}}}_{h}(s)\log\frac{KH}{\delta}}\geq\frac{K}{8}d^{\mu^{\mathsf{n}},\nu^{\mathsf{n}}}_{h}(s).`$ |  |

As a consequent,

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle N^{\mathsf{t}}_{h}(s)\mu_{h}^{\mathsf{n}}(a\,|\,s)\nu_{h}^{\mathsf{n}}(b\,|\,s)`$ | $`\displaystyle\geq\frac{K}{8}d^{\mu^{\mathsf{n}},\nu^{\mathsf{n}}}_{h}(s)\mu_{h}^{\mathsf{n}}(a\,|\,s)\nu_{h}^{\mathsf{n}}(b\,|\,s)`$ |  | (41) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\frac{K}{8}d^{\mu^{\mathsf{n}},\nu^{\mathsf{n}}}_{h}(s,a,b)\geq 200\log\frac{KH}{\delta},`$ |  | (42) |

where the last inequality holds under the assumption of the second case.
Combining
([41](#A3.E41 "In C.2 Proof of 𝑁^𝗍_ℎ⁢(𝑠)≤𝑁^𝗆_ℎ⁢(𝑠) ‣ Appendix C Proof of Lemma ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning"))
with
([38](#A3.E38 "In C.2 Proof of 𝑁^𝗍_ℎ⁢(𝑠)≤𝑁^𝗆_ℎ⁢(𝑠) ‣ Appendix C Proof of Lemma ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning"))
yields

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle N^{\mathsf{t}}_{h}(s,a,b)`$ | $`\displaystyle\geq\frac{K}{8}d^{\mu^{\mathsf{n}},\nu^{\mathsf{n}}}_{h}(s,a,b)-\sqrt{\frac{K}{2}d^{\mu^{\mathsf{n}},\nu^{\mathsf{n}}}_{h}(s,a,b)\log\frac{KH}{\delta}}-\log\frac{KH}{\delta}`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\geq\frac{K}{8}d^{\mu^{\mathsf{n}},\nu^{\mathsf{n}}}_{h}(s,a,b)-2\sqrt{Kd^{\mu^{\mathsf{n}},\nu^{\mathsf{n}}}_{h}(s,a,b)\log\frac{KH}{\delta}}.`$ |  |

Combining the result above with
([39](#A3.E39 "In C.2 Proof of 𝑁^𝗍_ℎ⁢(𝑠)≤𝑁^𝗆_ℎ⁢(𝑠) ‣ Appendix C Proof of Lemma ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning"))
and referencing
([38](#A3.E38 "In C.2 Proof of 𝑁^𝗍_ℎ⁢(𝑠)≤𝑁^𝗆_ℎ⁢(𝑠) ‣ Appendix C Proof of Lemma ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning")),
we conclude the proof of
Lemma [3.1](#S3.Thmtheorem1 "Lemma 3.1. ‣ 3.1 Building an Empirical Nominal MDP ‣ 3 Algorithm Design ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning").

### C.3 Proof of ([38](#A3.E38 "In C.2 Proof of 𝑁^𝗍_ℎ⁢(𝑠)≤𝑁^𝗆_ℎ⁢(𝑠) ‣ Appendix C Proof of Lemma ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning")).

To prove
([38](#A3.E38 "In C.2 Proof of 𝑁^𝗍_ℎ⁢(𝑠)≤𝑁^𝗆_ℎ⁢(𝑠) ‣ Appendix C Proof of Lemma ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning")),
we analyze two cases, i.e.,
$`N^{\mathsf{t}}_{h}(s)\mu_{h}^{\mathsf{n}}(a\,|\,s)\nu_{h}^{\mathsf{n}}(b\,|\,s)\leq 4\log\frac{KH}{\delta}`$
and
$`N^{\mathsf{t}}_{h}(s)\mu_{h}^{\mathsf{n}}(a\,|\,s)\nu_{h}^{\mathsf{n}}(b\,|\,s)>4\log\frac{KH}{\delta}`$.

In the first case of
$`N^{\mathsf{t}}_{h}(s)\mu_{h}^{\mathsf{n}}(a\,|\,s)\nu_{h}^{\mathsf{n}}(b\,|\,s)\leq 4\log\frac{KH}{\delta}`$,
we conclude the right-hand side of
([38](#A3.E38 "In C.2 Proof of 𝑁^𝗍_ℎ⁢(𝑠)≤𝑁^𝗆_ℎ⁢(𝑠) ‣ Appendix C Proof of Lemma ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning"))
is negative, leading to
([38](#A3.E38 "In C.2 Proof of 𝑁^𝗍_ℎ⁢(𝑠)≤𝑁^𝗆_ℎ⁢(𝑠) ‣ Appendix C Proof of Lemma ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning")).
In the second case of
$`N^{\mathsf{t}}_{h}(s)\mu_{h}^{\mathsf{n}}(a\,|\,s)\nu_{h}^{\mathsf{n}}(b\,|\,s)>4\log\frac{KH}{\delta}`$,
we compose a special set $`\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{l}}`$ as

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{l}}\coloneqq\bigg\{(s,a,b,h)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}\times[H]\,\,\Big|\,\,N^{\mathsf{t}}_{h}(s)\mu_{h}^{\mathsf{n}}(a\,|\,s)\nu_{h}^{\mathsf{n}}(b\,|\,s)>4\log\frac{KH}{\delta}\bigg\}.
``` |  | (43) |

Given the fact that

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\sum_{(s,a,b,h)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}\times[H]}N^{\mathsf{t}}_{h}(s)\mu_{h}^{\mathsf{n}}(a\,|\,s)\nu_{h}^{\mathsf{n}}(b\,|\,s)=`$ | $`\displaystyle\sum_{(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}\times[H]}N^{\mathsf{t}}_{h}(s)\sum_{(a,b)\in\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}}\mu_{h}^{\mathsf{n}}(a\,|\,s)\nu_{h}^{\mathsf{n}}(b\,|\,s)`$ |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle=`$ | $`\displaystyle\sum_{(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}\times[H]}N^{\mathsf{t}}_{h}(s)\leq\sum_{(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}\times[H]}N^{\mathsf{a}}_{h}(s)=\frac{KH}{2},`$ |  |

the cardinality of $`\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{l}}`$ can be bounded as:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\big|\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{l}}\big|<\frac{\sum_{(s,a,b,h)}N^{\mathsf{t}}_{h}(s)\mu_{h}^{\mathsf{n}}(a\,|\,s)\nu_{h}^{\mathsf{n}}(b\,|\,s)}{4\log\frac{KH}{\delta}}\leq KH/2.
``` |  | (44) |

Moreover, we can view $`N^{\mathsf{t}}_{h}(s,a)`$ as the sum of
$`N^{\mathsf{t}}_{h}(s)`$ independent Bernoulli random variables with
mean $`\mu_{h}^{\mathsf{n}}(a\mid s)\nu_{h}^{\mathsf{n}}(b\mid s)`$, due
to $`N^{\mathsf{t}}_{h}(s)\leq N^{\mathsf{m}}_{h}(s)`$ with high
probability and conditioned on $`N^{\mathsf{t}}_{h}(s)`$ and
$`N^{\mathsf{m}}_{h}(s)`$. Analogous to
([31](#A3.E31 "In C.2 Proof of 𝑁^𝗍_ℎ⁢(𝑠)≤𝑁^𝗆_ℎ⁢(𝑠) ‣ Appendix C Proof of Lemma ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning"))
based on the condition
$`N^{\mathsf{t}}_{h}(s)\leq N^{\mathsf{m}}_{h}(s)`$, we can repeat the
Bernstein-type argument and obtain that for any fixed triple
$`(s,a,b,h)`$, with probability of at least $`1-2\delta/(KH)`$,

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle N^{\mathsf{t}}_{h}(s,a,b)\geq`$ | $`\displaystyle N^{\mathsf{t}}_{h}(s)\mu_{h}^{\mathsf{n}}(a\,|\,s)\nu_{h}^{\mathsf{n}}(b\,|\,s)-\sqrt{4N^{\mathsf{t}}_{h}(s)\mu_{h}^{\mathsf{n}}(a\,|\,s)\nu_{h}^{\mathsf{n}}(b\,|\,s)\log\frac{KH}{\delta}}-\log\frac{KH}{\delta}.`$ |  | (45) |

With probability exceeding $`1-\delta`$,
([45](#A3.E45 "In C.3 Proof of (). ‣ Appendix C Proof of Lemma ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning"))
holds $`\forall(s,a,b,h)\in\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{l}}`$ by utilizing the
union bound of
([44](#A3.E44 "In C.3 Proof of (). ‣ Appendix C Proof of Lemma ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning"))
over all $`(s,a,b,h)\in\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{l}}`$. Combining the two
cases, we conclude that
([38](#A3.E38 "In C.2 Proof of 𝑁^𝗍_ℎ⁢(𝑠)≤𝑁^𝗆_ℎ⁢(𝑠) ‣ Appendix C Proof of Lemma ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning"))
holds
$`\forall(s,a,b,h)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}\times[H]`$
with probability of at least $`1-\delta`$.

## Appendix D Proof of Theorem [4.2](#S4.Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 4.2 (Upper bound for RTZ-VI-LCB). ‣ Robust unilateral clipped concentrability. ‣ 4 Performance Guarantees ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning")

Theorem [4.2](#S4.Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 4.2 (Upper bound for RTZ-VI-LCB). ‣ Robust unilateral clipped concentrability. ‣ 4 Performance Guarantees ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning")
can be proved in three steps.

### D.1 Step 1: Decoupling statistical dependency

Before bounding $`\mathrm{Gap}(\widehat{\mu},\widehat{\nu})`$, we
introduce an important lemma, quantifying the difference between
$`\widehat{P}`$ and $`P`$ when projected in the direction of the value
function.

###### Lemma D.1.

Instate the assumptions in
Theorem [4.2](#S4.Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 4.2 (Upper bound for RTZ-VI-LCB). ‣ Robust unilateral clipped concentrability. ‣ 4 Performance Guarantees ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning").
Consider any vector $`V\in\mathbb{R}^{S}`$ with $`\|V\|_{\infty}\leq H`$
for all
$`(h,s,a,b)\in[H]\times\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}`$
satisfying $`N_{h}\left(s,a,b\right)>0`$. With probability of at least
$`1-\delta`$, one has

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\left|\inf_{{P}\in\mathcal{U}^{\sigma^{+}}(\widehat{P}_{h,s,a,b}^{0})}\!\!PV\!-\!\inf_{{P}\in\mathcal{U}^{\sigma^{+}}(P^{0}_{h,s,a,b})}\!\!PV\right|\!\leq\!C_{4}\sqrt{\frac{1}{N_{h}\!\left(s,\!a,\!b\right)}\mathsf{Var}_{\widehat{P}^{0}_{h,s,a,b}}\!\!\big({V}\big)\!\log\!\frac{KH}{\delta}}\!+\!C_{4}\frac{H\log\!\frac{KH}{\delta}}{N_{h}\left(s,\!a,\!b\right)}`$ |  | (46) |

for some sufficiently large constant $`C_{4}>0`$, and

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathsf{Var}_{\widehat{P}^{0}_{h,s,a,b}}\big({V}\big)\leq 2\mathsf{Var}_{P^{0}_{h,s,a,b}}\big({V}\big)+O\left(\frac{H^{2}}{N_{h}\left(s,a,b\right)}\log\frac{KH}{\delta}\right).
``` |  | (47) |

Proof can be found in
Appendix [G.1](#A7.SS1 "G.1 Proof of Lemma ‣ Appendix G Proofs of lemmas for Theorem ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning").

In simple terms,
([46](#A4.E46 "In Lemma D.1. ‣ D.1 Step 1: Decoupling statistical dependency ‣ Appendix D Proof of Theorem ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning"))
provides a Bernstein-type concentration bound, while
([47](#A4.E47 "In Lemma D.1. ‣ D.1 Step 1: Decoupling statistical dependency ‣ Appendix D Proof of Theorem ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning"))
ensures that the empirical variance estimate (i.e., the plug-in
estimate) closely matches the true variance. Notably,
Lemma [D.1](#A4.Thmtheorem1 "Lemma D.1. ‣ D.1 Step 1: Decoupling statistical dependency ‣ Appendix D Proof of Theorem ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning")
does not require $`V`$ to be statistically independent of
$`\widehat{P}^{0}_{h,s,a,b}`$, which is essential given the complex
statistical dependencies in RTZ-VI-LCB. Under the leave-one-out analysis
(see, e.g., \[[1](#bib.bib37), [7](#bib.bib39), [24](#bib.bib47),
[25](#bib.bib38)\]), we prove
Lemma [D.1](#A4.Thmtheorem1 "Lemma D.1. ‣ D.1 Step 1: Decoupling statistical dependency ‣ Appendix D Proof of Theorem ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning")
to decouple statistical dependencies, as illustrated in
Appendix [G.1](#A7.SS1 "G.1 Proof of Lemma ‣ Appendix G Proofs of lemmas for Theorem ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning").

With
Lemma [D.1](#A4.Thmtheorem1 "Lemma D.1. ‣ D.1 Step 1: Decoupling statistical dependency ‣ Appendix D Proof of Theorem ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning"),
we now have: for any
$`(h,s,a,b)\in[H]\times\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}`$
satisfying $`N_{h}(s,a,b)\geq 1`$,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\left|\inf_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{U}^{\sigma^{+}}(\widehat{P}_{h,s,a,b}^{0})}PV-\inf_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{U}^{\sigma^{+}}(P^{0}_{h,s,a,b})}PV\right|\leq\beta_{h}\left(s,a,b,{V}\right).
``` |  | (48) |

Therefore, we conclude that $`\widehat{Q}_{h}^{+}(s,a,b)`$ is an
optimistic estimation of
$`\widehat{Q}_{h}^{\mu,\nu,\sigma^{+}}(s,a,b)`$, as summarized below.

###### Lemma D.2.

With probability exceeding $`1-\delta`$, it holds that

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\widehat{Q}_{h}^{+}(s,a,b)\geq{Q}_{h}^{\star,\widehat{\nu},\sigma^{+}}(s,a,b)\qquad`$ | $`\displaystyle\text{and}\qquad\widehat{V}_{h}^{+}(s)\geq{V}_{h}^{\star,\widehat{\nu},\sigma^{+}}(s);`$ |  | (49) |

See
Appendix [G.2](#A7.SS2 "G.2 Proof of Lemma ‣ Appendix G Proofs of lemmas for Theorem ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning")
for detailed proofs.

Moreover, we introduce another key lemma highlighting the difference
between RTZMGs and standard TZMGs from the same idea of Lemma 3 in
\[[34](#bib.bib31)\]. The range of the robust value function narrows as
the uncertainty level $`\sigma^{+}`$ of its uncertainty set increases,
as shown below.

###### Lemma D.3.

Consider the uncertainty set $`\mathcal{U}^{\sigma^{+}}(\cdot)`$ with TV
distance and any RTZMG
$`\mathcal{MG}_{\mathsf{r}}=\big\{\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{U}^{\sigma^{+}}(P),\mathcal{U}^{\sigma^{-}}(P),r,H\big\}`$.
The optimistic robust value function estimate $`\widehat{V}_{h}^{+}`$:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\forall h\in[H]:\quad\max_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\widehat{V}_{h}^{+}-\min_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\widehat{V}_{h}^{+}`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq\min\left\{\frac{\left(H+1\right)\left(1-(1-\sigma^{+})^{H-h}\right)}{\sigma^{+}},H\right\}.`$ |  |

See
Appendix [G.3](#A7.SS3 "G.3 Proof of Lemma ‣ Appendix G Proofs of lemmas for Theorem ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning")
for detail proofs.

### D.2 Step 2: Decomposing the error $`\mathrm{Gap}(\widehat{\mu},\widehat{\nu})`$

The goal of RTZ-VI-LCB is to output an $`\varepsilon`$-robust NE policy
$`(\widehat{\mu},\widehat{\nu})`$ satisfying
$`\mathrm{Gap}(\widehat{\mu},\widehat{\nu})`$ in
([9](#S2.E9 "In Robust Nash equilibrium. ‣ 2 Problem Formulation ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning")),
i.e.,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathrm{Gap}(\widehat{\mu},\widehat{\nu})\coloneqq\max\left\{V_{1}^{\star,\widehat{\nu},\sigma^{+}}(\varrho)-V_{1}^{\star,\sigma^{+}}(\varrho),~V_{1}^{\star,\sigma^{-}}(\varrho)-V_{1}^{\widehat{\mu},\star,\sigma^{-}}(\varrho)\right\}\leq\varepsilon.`$ |  |

Due to the interchangeability between the max-player and the min-player,
we assume without loss of generality that
$`V_{1}^{\star,\widehat{\nu},\sigma^{+}}(\varrho)-V_{1}^{\star,\sigma^{+}}(\varrho)`$
is larger than
$`V_{1}^{\star,\sigma^{-}}(\varrho)-V_{1}^{\widehat{\mu},\star,\sigma^{-}}(\varrho)`$,
leading to
$`\mathrm{Gap}(\widehat{\mu},\widehat{\nu})\leq\left\{V_{1}^{\star,\widehat{\nu},\sigma^{+}}(\varrho)-V_{1}^{\star,\sigma^{+}}(\varrho)\right\}`$.

According to the relationship in
Lemma [D.2](#A4.Thmtheorem2 "Lemma D.2. ‣ D.1 Step 1: Decoupling statistical dependency ‣ Appendix D Proof of Theorem ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning"),
we obtain

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle{V}_{h}^{\star,\widehat{\nu},\sigma^{+}}(s)\leq\widehat{V}_{h}^{+}(s)=`$ | $`\displaystyle\max_{{\mu}\in\Delta(\mathcal{A})}\min_{{\nu}\in\Delta(\mathcal{B})}\mathbb{E}_{(a,b)\sim({\mu}(s),{\nu}(s))}\left[\widehat{Q}_{h}^{+}(s,a,b)\right]`$ |  |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle\leq`$ | $`\displaystyle\max_{{\mu}\in\Delta(\mathcal{A})}\mathbb{E}_{(a,b)\sim({\mu}(s),{\nu}^{\star}(s))}\left[{Q}_{h}^{+}(s,a,b)\right],`$ |  | (50) |

where the first equality comes from line 5 in
Algorithm [2](#alg2 "Algorithm 2 ‣ Policy estimation. ‣ 3.2 Optimistic Variant of Robust Value Iteration with Lower Confidence Bounds ‣ 3 Algorithm Design ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning").
Therefore, there exists a deterministic policy
$`\mu^{\sf{d}}:\mathcal{S}\leftarrow\Delta(\mathcal{A})`$ satisfying
that for any $`s\in\mathcal{S}`$

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mu^{\sf{d}}(s)\coloneqq\arg\max_{{\mu}\in\Delta(\mathcal{A})}\mathbb{E}_{(a,b)\sim({\mu}(s),{\nu}^{\star}(s))}\left[{Q}_{h}^{+}(s,a,b)\right].`$ |  | (51) |

We start by defining the following notation:

- •
  The state-action space covered by the behavior policy
  $`(\mu^{\mathsf{n}},\nu^{\mathsf{n}})`$ in the nominal transition
  kernel $`P^{0}`$ is denoted as

  |  |  |  |  |
  |----|----|----|----|
  |  | $`\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{n}}=\left\{(h,s,a,b):d_{h}^{\mathsf{n}}(s,a,b)>0\right\}.`$ |  | (52) |
- •
  For any time step $`h\in[H]`$, the sets of potential state occupancy
  distributions w.r.t. the policy $`(\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),\nu^{\star}(s))`$
  and the uncertainty set
  $`P\in\mathcal{U}^{\sigma^{+}}\left(P^{0}\right)`$ are given by

  |  |  |  |  |  |
  |----|----|----|----|----|
  |  | $`\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{p}}_{h}`$ | $`\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{\left[d_{h}^{\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),\nu^{\star}(s),P}(s)\right]_{s\in\mathcal{S}}:P\in\mathcal{U}^{\sigma^{+}}\left(P^{0}\right)\right\};`$ |  | (53) |
  |  | $`\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{pa}}_{h}`$ | $`\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{\left[d_{h}^{\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),\nu^{\star}(s),P}(s,a,b)\right]_{(s,a,b)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}}:P\in\mathcal{U}^{\sigma^{+}}\left(P^{0}\right)\right\}.`$ |  | (54) |
- •
  For convenience and without ambiguity, we introduce additional
  notation for $`h\in[H]`$ as

  |  |  |  |
  |----|----|----|
  |  |
  ``` math
  \beta_{h}^{\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s)=\mathbb{E}_{(a,b)\sim(\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),\nu^{\star}(s))}\beta_{h}\left(s,a,b,\widehat{V}_{h+1}^{+}\right).
  ``` |  |

  In particular, the vector
  $`\beta_{h}^{\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}\in\mathbb{R}^{S}`$ is defined
  with its $`s`$-th term given by
  $`\beta_{h}^{\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s)`$.
- •
  Similarly, we can define the notation related to rewards for
  $`h\in[H]`$ as

  |  |  |  |
  |----|----|----|
  |  |
  ``` math
  \widehat{r}_{h}^{\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s)=\mathbb{E}_{(a,b)\sim(\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),\nu^{\star}(s))}\widehat{r}_{h}\left(s,a,b\right).
  ``` |  |

According to the update rule in line 4 in
Algorithm [2](#alg2 "Algorithm 2 ‣ Policy estimation. ‣ 3.2 Optimistic Variant of Robust Value Iteration with Lower Confidence Bounds ‣ 3 Algorithm Design ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning")
and robust Bellman equality
([28](#A2.E28 "In B.2 Facts of RTZMGs and empirical RTZMGs ‣ Appendix B Preliminaries ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning")),
we derive

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle V_{h}^{\star,\widehat{\nu},\sigma^{+}}(s)-V_{h}^{\star,\sigma^{+}}(s)`$ |  | (55) |
|  | $`\displaystyle\leq`$ | $`\displaystyle\widehat{V}_{h}^{+}(s)-{V}_{h}^{{\mu}^{\sf{d}},{\nu}^{\star},\sigma^{+}}(s)`$ |  |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle\leq`$ | $`\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{(a,b)\sim(\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),\nu^{\star}(s))}\inf_{P\in\mathcal{U}^{\sigma^{+}}\big(\widehat{P}_{h,s,a,b}^{0}\big)}P\widehat{V}_{h+1}^{+}+\beta_{h}^{\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s)`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle-\mathbb{E}_{(a,b)\sim(\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),\nu^{\star}(s))}\inf_{P\in\mathcal{U}^{\sigma^{+}}\left({P}^{0}_{h,s,a,b}\right)}P{V}_{h+1}^{{\mu}^{\sf{d}},{\nu}^{\star},\sigma^{+}}`$ |  |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle\leq`$ | $`\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{(a,b)\sim(\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),\nu^{\star}(s))}\Bigg[\inf_{P\in\mathcal{U}^{\sigma^{+}}\big({P}_{h,s,a,b}^{0}\big)}P\widehat{V}_{h+1}^{+}-\inf_{P\in\mathcal{U}^{\sigma^{+}}\left({P}^{0}_{h,s,a,b}\right)}P{V}_{h+1}^{{\mu}^{\sf{d}},{\nu}^{\star},\sigma^{+}}`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle+\left|\inf_{P\in\mathcal{U}^{\sigma^{+}}\left({P}^{0}_{h,s,a,b}\right)}\!P\widehat{V}_{h+1}^{+}-\inf_{P\in\mathcal{U}^{\sigma^{+}}\big(\widehat{P}_{h,s,a,b}^{0}\big)}P\widehat{V}_{h+1}^{+}\right|\Bigg]+\beta_{h}^{\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s)`$ |  |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(i)}}{\leq}`$ | $`\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{(a,b)\sim(\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),\nu^{\star}(s))}\left[\inf_{P\in\mathcal{U}^{\sigma^{+}}\big({P}_{h,s,a,b}^{0}\big)}P\widehat{V}_{h+1}^{+}-\inf_{P\in\mathcal{U}^{\sigma^{+}}\left({P}^{0}_{h,s,a,b}\right)}P{V}_{h+1}^{{\mu}^{\sf{d}},{\nu}^{\star},\sigma^{+}}\right]+2\beta_{h}^{\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s)`$ |  |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(ii)}}{\leq}`$ | $`\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{(a,b)\sim(\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),\nu^{\star}(s))}\left[{P}^{\inf,V}_{h,s,a,b}\left(\widehat{V}_{h+1}^{+}-{V}_{h+1}^{{\mu}^{\sf{d}},{\nu}^{\star},\sigma^{+}}\right)\right]+2\beta_{h}^{\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s).`$ |  | (56) |

Here, (i) holds due to
([48](#A4.E48 "In D.1 Step 1: Decoupling statistical dependency ‣ Appendix D Proof of Theorem ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning"))
in
Lemma [D.1](#A4.Thmtheorem1 "Lemma D.1. ‣ D.1 Step 1: Decoupling statistical dependency ‣ Appendix D Proof of Theorem ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning")
for $`N_{h}(s,a,b)>0`$ and

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\left|\inf_{P\in\mathcal{U}^{\sigma^{+}}\left({P}^{0}_{h,s,a,b}\right)}\!P\widehat{V}_{h+1}^{+}-\inf_{P\in\mathcal{U}^{\sigma^{+}}\big(\widehat{P}_{h,s,a,b}^{0}\big)}P\widehat{V}_{h+1}^{+}\right|\leq H=\beta_{h}^{\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s)\text{ for }N_{h}(s,a,b)=0.`$ |  | (57) |

Moreover, (ii) is due to

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle{P}^{\inf,V}_{h,s,a,b}\coloneqq\mathrm{argmin}_{P\in\mathcal{U}^{\sigma^{+}}\big(P^{0}_{h,s,a,b}\big)}{P}{V}_{h+1}^{{\mu}^{\sf{d}},{\nu}^{\star},\sigma^{+}}`$ |  | (58) |

and consequently,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\inf_{P\in\mathcal{U}^{\sigma^{+}}\left({P}^{0}_{h,s,a,b}\right)}P{V}_{h+1}^{{\mu}^{\sf{d}},{\nu}^{\star},\sigma^{+}}={P}^{\inf,V}_{h,s,a,b}{V}_{h+1}^{{\mu}^{\sf{d}},{\nu}^{\star},\sigma^{+}},\mbox{ and }\inf_{P\in\mathcal{U}^{\sigma^{+}}\left({P}^{0}_{h,s,a,b}\right)}P\widehat{V}_{h+1}^{+}\leq{P}^{\inf,V}_{h,s,a,b}\widehat{V}_{h+1}^{+}.`$ |  |

For ease of exposure, we introduce notation as
$`\widetilde{P}^{\inf,V}_{h,s}\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{(a,b)\sim(\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),\nu^{\star}(s))}{P}^{\inf,V}_{h,s,a,b}`$.
Furthermore, we define a sequence of matrices
$`\widetilde{P}^{\inf,V}_{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{S\times S}`$. We can utilize
([56](#A4.E56 "In D.2 Step 2: Decomposing the error Gap(𝜇̂,𝜈̂) ‣ Appendix D Proof of Theorem ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning"))
recursively over the time steps $`h,h+1,\cdots,H`$ and derive

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle V_{h}^{\star,\widehat{\nu},\sigma^{+}}(s)-V_{h}^{\star,\sigma^{+}}(s)`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq\widehat{V}_{h}^{+}(s)-{V}_{h}^{{\mu}^{\sf{d}},{\nu}^{\star},\sigma^{+}}(s)`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq\widetilde{P}^{\inf,V}_{h}\left(\widehat{V}_{h+1}^{+}-{V}_{h+1}^{{\mu}^{\sf{d}},{\nu}^{\star},\sigma^{+}}\right)+2\beta_{h}^{\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s)`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq\widetilde{P}^{\inf,V}_{h}\widetilde{P}^{\inf,V}_{h+1}\left(\widehat{V}_{h+2}^{+}-{V}_{h+2}^{{\mu}^{\sf{d}},{\nu}^{\star},\sigma^{+}}\right)+2\widetilde{P}^{\inf,V}_{h}\beta_{h+1}^{{\mu}^{\sf{d}},{\nu}^{\star}}+2\beta_{h}^{\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s)`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq\cdots\leq 2\sum_{i=h+1}^{H}\left(\prod_{j=h}^{i-1}\widetilde{P}^{\inf,V}_{j}\right)\beta_{i}^{\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}+2\beta_{h}^{\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s)`$ |  | (59) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=2\sum_{i=h}^{H}\left(\prod_{j=h}^{i-1}\widetilde{P}^{\inf,V}_{j}\right)\beta_{i}^{\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}},`$ |  | (60) |

For conciseness, we define
$`\left(\prod_{j=h}^{h-1}\widetilde{P}^{\inf,V}_{j}\right)=I`$ to
simplify notation.

For any
$`d_{h}^{\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}\in\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{p}}_{h}`$
(cf. ([53](#A4.E53 "In 2nd item ‣ D.2 Step 2: Decomposing the error Gap(𝜇̂,𝜈̂) ‣ Appendix D Proof of Theorem ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning"))),
taking inner product with
([59](#A4.Ex33 "In D.2 Step 2: Decomposing the error Gap(𝜇̂,𝜈̂) ‣ Appendix D Proof of Theorem ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning"))
yields

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\left<d_{h}^{\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}},V_{h}^{\star,\widehat{\nu},\sigma^{+}}-V_{h}^{\star,\sigma^{+}}\right>`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq\left<d_{h}^{\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}},2\sum_{i=h}^{H}\left(\prod_{j=h}^{i-1}\widetilde{P}^{\inf,V}_{j}\right)\beta_{i}^{\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}\right>=2\sum_{i=h}^{H}\left\langle d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}},\beta_{i}^{\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}\right\rangle,`$ |  | (61) |

where

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}\coloneqq\left[\big(d_{h}^{\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}\big)^{\top}\left(\prod_{j=h}^{i-1}\widetilde{P}^{\inf,V}_{j}\right)\right]^{\top}\in\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{p}}_{i}`$ |  | (62) |

by the definition of $`\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{p}}_{i}`$
(cf. ([53](#A4.E53 "In 2nd item ‣ D.2 Step 2: Decomposing the error Gap(𝜇̂,𝜈̂) ‣ Appendix D Proof of Theorem ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning")))
for all $`i=h+1,\cdots,H`$.

Next, we control
$`\langle d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}},\beta_{i}^{\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}\rangle`$
by using concentrability. According to
([18](#S3.E18 "In Penalty term. ‣ 3.2 Optimistic Variant of Robust Value Iteration with Lower Confidence Bounds ‣ 3 Algorithm Design ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning"))
in
Lemma [D.1](#A4.Thmtheorem1 "Lemma D.1. ‣ D.1 Step 1: Decoupling statistical dependency ‣ Appendix D Proof of Theorem ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning"),
we first demonstrate that the pessimistic penalty satisfies

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\beta_{i}(s,a,b,\hat{V})`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq\max\left\{\sqrt{\frac{C_{\mathsf{n}}\log\frac{KH}{\delta}}{N_{i}\left(s,a,b\right)}\mathsf{Var}_{\widehat{P}^{0}_{i,s,a,b}}(\widehat{V})},\frac{2C_{\mathsf{n}}H\log\frac{KH}{\delta}}{N_{i}\left(s,a,b\right)}\right\}`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{C_{\mathsf{n}}\log\frac{KH}{\delta}}{N_{i}\left(s,a,b\right)}\mathsf{Var}_{\widehat{P}^{0}_{i,s,a,b}}(\widehat{V})}+\frac{2C_{\mathsf{n}}H\log\frac{KH}{\delta}}{N_{i}\left(s,a,b\right)}`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(i)}}{\leq}\sqrt{\frac{C_{\mathsf{n}}\log\frac{KH}{\delta}}{N_{i}\left(s,a,b\right)}\left(2\mathsf{Var}_{P^{0}_{i,s,a,b}}\big({\widehat{V}}\big)+\frac{C_{0}H^{2}}{N_{i}\left(s,a,b\right)}\log\frac{KH}{\delta}\right)}+\frac{2C_{\mathsf{n}}H\log\frac{KH}{\delta}}{N_{i}\left(s,a,b\right)}`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(ii)}}{\leq}\sqrt{\frac{2C_{\mathsf{n}}\log\frac{KH}{\delta}}{N_{i}\left(s,a,b\right)}\mathsf{Var}_{P^{0}_{i,s,a,b}}\big({\widehat{V}}\big)}+\frac{\left(2C_{\mathsf{n}}+\sqrt{C_{\mathsf{n}}C_{0}}\right)H\log\frac{KH}{\delta}}{N_{i}\left(s,a,b\right)}`$ |  | (63) |

where (i) holds by applying
([47](#A4.E47 "In Lemma D.1. ‣ D.1 Step 1: Decoupling statistical dependency ‣ Appendix D Proof of Theorem ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning"))
for some sufficiently large $`C_{0}`$ and (ii) follows from the
Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. Therefore, combining the definition of
$`\beta_{i}^{\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s)`$, we obtain

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\langle d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}},\beta_{i}^{\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}\rangle=`$ | $`\displaystyle\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s)\beta_{i}^{\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s)`$ |  |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle=`$ | $`\displaystyle\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s)\mathbb{E}_{(a,b)\sim(\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),\nu^{\star}(s))}\beta_{i}(s,a,b,\hat{V})`$ |  |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle=`$ | $`\displaystyle\sum_{(s,a,b)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}}d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s)\ind\{a={\mu}^{\sf{d}}(s)\}{\nu}^{\star}(b|s)\beta_{i}(s,a,b,\hat{V})`$ |  |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle=`$ | $`\displaystyle\sum_{(s,b)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{B}}d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b)\beta_{i}(s,{\mu}^{\sf{d}}(s),b,\hat{V}),`$ |  | (64) |

where the last equation holds due to the definition in
([5](#S2.E5 "In Offline dataset. ‣ 2 Problem Formulation ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning")).
Then, we observe
$`d_{h}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s,a,b)\in\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{pa}}_{h}`$;
cf. ([54](#A4.E54 "In 2nd item ‣ D.2 Step 2: Decomposing the error Gap(𝜇̂,𝜈̂) ‣ Appendix D Proof of Theorem ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning")).
Thereafter, we divide the bound
([64](#A4.Ex39 "In D.2 Step 2: Decomposing the error Gap(𝜇̂,𝜈̂) ‣ Appendix D Proof of Theorem ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning"))
into two cases. In the first case, i.e., $`s\in S`$ where
$`\max_{P\in\mathcal{U}^{\sigma^{+}}(P^{0})}d_{i}^{\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star},P}\big(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b\big)=0`$,
it follows from the
definition ([53](#A4.E53 "In 2nd item ‣ D.2 Step 2: Decomposing the error Gap(𝜇̂,𝜈̂) ‣ Appendix D Proof of Theorem ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning"))
that: For any
$`d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b)\in\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{pa}}_{i}`$,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b)=0.`$ |  | (65) |

In the second case, i.e., $`s\in S`$ where
$`\max_{P\in\mathcal{U}^{\sigma^{+}}(P^{0})}d_{i}^{\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star},P}\big(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b\big)>0`$,
under the assumption
in ([20](#S4.Ex4 "In Definition 4.1 (Robust unilateral clipped concentrability). ‣ Robust unilateral clipped concentrability. ‣ 4 Performance Guarantees ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning")),

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\max_{P\in\mathcal{U}^{\sigma^{+}}(P^{0})}\frac{\min\big\{d_{i}^{\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star},P}\big(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b\big),\frac{1}{S(A+B)}\big\}}{d^{\mathsf{n}}_{i}\big(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b\big)}\leq C_{\mathsf{r}}^{\star}<\infty,`$ |  |

which implies that

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle d^{\mathsf{n}}_{i}\big(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b\big)>0\quad\text{and}\quad\big(i,s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b\big)\in\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{n}}.`$ |  | (66) |

Lemma [3.1](#S3.Thmtheorem1 "Lemma 3.1. ‣ 3.1 Building an Empirical Nominal MDP ‣ 3 Algorithm Design ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning")
tells that with probability of at least $`1-8\delta`$,

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle N_{i}\big(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b\big)`$ | $`\displaystyle\geq\frac{Kd^{\mathsf{n}}_{i}\big(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b\big)}{8}-5\sqrt{Kd^{\mathsf{n}}_{i}\big(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b\big)\log\frac{KH}{\delta}}`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(i)}}{\geq}\frac{Kd^{\mathsf{n}}_{i}\big(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b\big)}{16}`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(ii)}}{\geq}\frac{K\max_{P\in\mathcal{U}^{\sigma}(P^{0})}\min\left\{d_{i}^{\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star},P}\big(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b\big),\frac{1}{S(A+B)}\right\}}{16C_{\mathsf{r}}^{\star}}`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\geq\frac{K\min\left\{d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b),\frac{1}{S(A+B)}\right\}}{16C_{\mathsf{r}}^{\star}},`$ |  | (67) |

Here, (i) holds since, with
$`f(\sigma^{+},\sigma^{-},H)=\min\left\{\frac{H\sigma^{+}+1-(1-\sigma^{+})^{H}}{(\sigma^{+})^{2}},\frac{H\sigma^{-}+1-(1-\sigma^{-})^{H}}{(\sigma^{-})^{2}},H\right\}`$,

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle Kd^{\mathsf{n}}_{i}\big(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b\big)`$ | $`\displaystyle\geq c_{0}\frac{HS(A+B)}{d_{\mathsf{m}}^{\mathsf{n}}}\log\frac{KH}{\delta}f(\sigma^{+},\sigma^{-},H)d^{\mathsf{n}}_{i}\big(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b\big)`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\geq c_{0}HS(A+B)\log\frac{KH}{\delta}f(\sigma^{+},\sigma^{-},H)\geq 1600\log\frac{KH}{\delta},`$ |  | (68) |

where the first inequality follows from condition
([22](#S4.E22 "In Theorem 4.2 (Upper bound for RTZ-VI-LCB). ‣ Robust unilateral clipped concentrability. ‣ 4 Performance Guarantees ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning")),
and the second inequality follows from

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
d_{\mathsf{m}}^{\mathsf{n}}=\min_{h,s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b}\left\{d^{\mathsf{n}}_{h}(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b):d^{\mathsf{n}}_{h}(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b)>0\right\}\leq d^{\mathsf{n}}_{i}\big(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b\big).
``` |  | (69) |

Moreover, (ii) comes from
Assumption [4.1](#S4.Thmtheorem1 "Definition 4.1 (Robust unilateral clipped concentrability). ‣ Robust unilateral clipped concentrability. ‣ 4 Performance Guarantees ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning").

Combining
([63](#A4.Ex36 "In D.2 Step 2: Decomposing the error Gap(𝜇̂,𝜈̂) ‣ Appendix D Proof of Theorem ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning"))
and
([64](#A4.Ex39 "In D.2 Step 2: Decomposing the error Gap(𝜇̂,𝜈̂) ‣ Appendix D Proof of Theorem ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning")),
we arrive at

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\langle d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}},\beta_{i}^{\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}\rangle`$ |  |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle=`$ | $`\displaystyle\sum_{(s,b)in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{B}}d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b)\beta_{i}(s,{\mu}^{\sf{d}}(s),b,\hat{V})`$ |  |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle\leq`$ | $`\displaystyle\sum_{(s,b)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{B}}d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b)\sqrt{\frac{2C_{\mathsf{n}}\log\frac{KH}{\delta}}{N_{i}\left(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b\right)}\mathsf{Var}_{P^{0}_{i,s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b}}\big({\widehat{V}}\big)}`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle+\sum_{(s,b)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{B}}d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b)\frac{\left(2C_{\mathsf{n}}+\sqrt{C_{\mathsf{n}}C_{0}}\right)H\log\frac{KH}{\delta}}{N_{i}\left(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b\right)}`$ |  |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(i)}}{\leq}`$ | $`\displaystyle\sum_{(s,b)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{B}}d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b)\underbrace{\sqrt{\frac{32C_{\mathsf{r}}^{\star}C_{\mathsf{n}}\log\frac{KH}{\delta}}{K\min\left\{d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b),\frac{1}{S(A+B)}\right\}}\mathsf{Var}_{P^{0}_{i,s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b}}\big({\widehat{V}}\big)}}_{B_{1}}`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle+\sum_{(s,b)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{B}}d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b)\underbrace{\frac{16C_{\mathsf{r}}^{\star}\left(2C_{\mathsf{n}}+\sqrt{C_{\mathsf{n}}C_{0}}\right)H\log\frac{KH}{\delta}}{K\min\left\{d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b),\frac{1}{S(A+B)}\right\}}}_{B_{2}}.`$ |  | (70) |

From
([61](#A4.E61 "In D.2 Step 2: Decomposing the error Gap(𝜇̂,𝜈̂) ‣ Appendix D Proof of Theorem ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning")),
we need to bound
$`\sum_{i=1}^{H}\sum_{(s,b)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{B}}d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b)B_{1}`$
and
$`\sum_{i=1}^{H}\sum_{(s,b)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{B}}d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b)B_{2}`$:

#### D.2.1 Bounding $`\sum_{i=1}^{H}\sum_{(s,b)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{B}}d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b)B_{1}`$

Combining
([68](#A4.Ex46 "In D.2 Step 2: Decomposing the error Gap(𝜇̂,𝜈̂) ‣ Appendix D Proof of Theorem ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning"))
with
$`\sum_{i=1}^{H}\sum_{(s,b)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{B}}d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b)B_{1}`$
yields

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{H}\sum_{(s,b)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{B}}d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b)B_{1}`$ |  |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle=`$ | $`\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{H}\sum_{(s,b)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{B}}d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b)\sqrt{\frac{32C_{\mathsf{r}}^{\star}C_{\mathsf{n}}\log\frac{KH}{\delta}}{K\min\left\{d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b),\frac{1}{S(A+B)}\right\}}\mathsf{Var}_{P^{0}_{i,s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b}}\big({\widehat{V}}\big)}`$ |  |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle\leq`$ | $`\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{H}\sum_{(s,b)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{B}}d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b)\times`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\max\Bigg\{\sqrt{\frac{32C_{\mathsf{r}}^{\star}C_{\mathsf{n}}\log\frac{KH}{\delta}}{Kd_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b)}\mathsf{Var}_{P^{0}_{i,s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b}}\big({\widehat{V}}\big)},\sqrt{\frac{32C_{\mathsf{r}}^{\star}C_{\mathsf{n}}S(A+B)\log\frac{KH}{\delta}}{K}\mathsf{Var}_{P^{0}_{i,s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b}}\big({\widehat{V}}\big)}\Bigg\},`$ |  | (71) |

Therefore, we have

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{H}\sum_{(s,b)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{B}}d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b)B_{1}`$ |  |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle\leq`$ | $`\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{H}\sum_{(s,b)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{B}}\sqrt{\frac{32C_{\mathsf{r}}^{\star}C_{\mathsf{n}}\log\frac{KH}{\delta}}{K}d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b)\mathsf{Var}_{P^{0}_{i,s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b}}\big({\widehat{V}}\big)}`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{H}\sum_{(s,b)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{B}}d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b)\sqrt{\frac{32C_{\mathsf{r}}^{\star}C_{\mathsf{n}}S(A+B)\log\frac{KH}{\delta}}{K}\mathsf{Var}_{P^{0}_{i,s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b}}\big({\widehat{V}}\big)}`$ |  |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle\leq`$ | $`\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{32C_{\mathsf{r}}^{\star}C_{\mathsf{n}}S(A+B)\log\frac{KH}{\delta}}{K}}\Bigg(\sqrt{H\sum_{i=1}^{H}\sum_{(s,b)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{B}}d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b)\mathsf{Var}_{P^{0}_{i,s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b}}\big({\widehat{V}}\big)}`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle+\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{H}\sum_{(s,b)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{B}}d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b)\mathsf{Var}_{P^{0}_{i,s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b}}\big({\widehat{V}}\big)}\times\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{H}\sum_{(s,b)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{B}}d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b)}\Bigg)`$ |  |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle=`$ | $`\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{128C_{\mathsf{r}}^{\star}C_{\mathsf{n}}HS(A+B)\log\frac{KH}{\delta}}{K}\sum_{i=1}^{H}\sum_{(s,b)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{B}}d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b)\mathsf{Var}_{P^{0}_{i,s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b}}\big({\widehat{V}}\big)},`$ |  | (72) |

where the last inequality follows from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.

Then, we introduce the following lemma about
$`\sum_{i=1}^{H}\sum\limits_{(s,b)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{B}}d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b)\mathsf{Var}_{P_{i,s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b}}\big({\widehat{V}}\big)`$,
with its proof in
Appendix [G.4](#A7.SS4 "G.4 Proof of Lemma ‣ Appendix G Proofs of lemmas for Theorem ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning").

###### Lemma D.4.

Considering $`\forall\delta\in(0,1)`$, with probability at least
$`1-\delta`$, one has: for any product policy
$`(\widehat{\mu},\widehat{\nu})`$,

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{H}\sum_{(s,b)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{B}}d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b)\mathsf{Var}_{P_{i,s,a,b}^{0}}\big({\widehat{V}_{i+1}}\big)\leq H\min\left\{\frac{2(H\sigma^{+}-1+(1-\sigma^{+})^{H})}{(\sigma^{+})^{2}},H\right\}`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\qquad\times\left(4\sum_{i=1}^{H}\sum_{(s,b)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{B}}d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b)\beta_{i}(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b,\widehat{V})+(H+3)\right).`$ |  | (73) |

Armed with
Lemma [D.4](#A4.Thmtheorem4 "Lemma D.4. ‣ D.2.1 Bounding ∑_{𝑖=1}^𝐻∑_{(𝑠,𝑏)∈𝒮×ℬ}{𝑑_𝑖^{𝗉,𝜇^𝖽,𝜈^⋆}⁢(𝑠,𝜇^𝖽⁢(𝑠),𝑏)⁢𝐵_1} ‣ D.2 Step 2: Decomposing the error Gap(𝜇̂,𝜈̂) ‣ Appendix D Proof of Theorem ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning"),
([72](#A4.Ex55 "In D.2.1 Bounding ∑_{𝑖=1}^𝐻∑_{(𝑠,𝑏)∈𝒮×ℬ}{𝑑_𝑖^{𝗉,𝜇^𝖽,𝜈^⋆}⁢(𝑠,𝜇^𝖽⁢(𝑠),𝑏)⁢𝐵_1} ‣ D.2 Step 2: Decomposing the error Gap(𝜇̂,𝜈̂) ‣ Appendix D Proof of Theorem ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning"))
can be further bounded as

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{H}\sum_{(s,b)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{B}}d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b)B_{1}`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{128C_{\mathsf{r}}^{\star}C_{\mathsf{n}}HS(A+B)\log\frac{KH}{\delta}}{K}}\sqrt{H\min\left\{\frac{2(H\sigma^{+}-1+(1-\sigma^{+})^{H})}{(\sigma^{+})^{2}},H\right\}}`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\qquad\times\sqrt{\left(4\sum_{i=1}^{H}\sum_{(s,b)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{B}}d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b)\beta_{i}(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b,\widehat{V})+(H+3)\right)}.`$ |  | (74) |

#### D.2.2 Bounding $`\sum_{i=1}^{H}\sum_{(s,b)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{B}}d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b)B_{2}`$

Combining
([67](#A4.E67 "In D.2 Step 2: Decomposing the error Gap(𝜇̂,𝜈̂) ‣ Appendix D Proof of Theorem ‣ Sample-Efficient Tabular Self-Play forOffline Robust Reinforcement Learning"))
with
$`\sum_{i=1}^{H}\sum_{(s,b)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{B}}d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b)B_{2}`$
yields

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{H}\sum_{(s,b)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{B}}d_{i}^{\sf{p},\mu^{\sf{d}},\nu^{\star}}(s,\mu^{\sf{d}}(s),b)B_{2}`$ |  |  |
````
